Financial Management, Capital Budgeting & Project Management (CPM/PERT)

6 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

Question 21 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Descriptive ID: MGT-Q21

Q21. Explain the scope of financial management. Compare profit maximization and wealth maximization. Describe sources of long-term and short-term capital and financial statements. Q21. Explain the scope of financial management. Compare profit maximization and wealth maximization. Describe sources of long-term and short-term capital and financial statements. Q21. Explain the scope of financial management. Compare profit maximization and wealth maximization. Describe sources of long-term and short-term capital and financial statements.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: December 2023 University Exam (HUT300) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 19(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Equity}, \quad W = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0$$

Wealth Maximization: Superior to profit maximization by discounting cash flows.
Capital Sources: Equity shares, debentures, retained earnings, term loans.
Financial Statements: Balance sheet and P&L income statement.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Equity}, \quad W = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0$$

Wealth Maximization: Superior to profit maximization by discounting cash flows.
Capital Sources: Equity shares, debentures, retained earnings, term loans.
Financial Statements: Balance sheet and P&L income statement.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Equity}, \quad W = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0$$

Wealth Maximization: Superior to profit maximization by discounting cash flows.
Capital Sources: Equity shares, debentures, retained earnings, term loans.
Financial Statements: Balance sheet and P&L income statement.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Financial management is the managerial activity concerned with the planning, procurement, allocation, and control of an enterprise's financial resources to maximize the economic value of the firm.

1. Primary Objectives: Profit Maximization vs Wealth Maximization: - Profit Maximization: - Principle: The traditional economic objective asserting that the primary goal of business decisions is to maximize accounting net profit. - Limitations: Ignores the Time Value of Money ($INR 100$ earned today is worth more than $INR 100$ earned five years hence); ignores Business and Financial Risk (incentivizes high-risk, volatile projects that report high nominal profits); relies on accounting profit which is vulnerable to manipulation; overlooks dividend policies and long-term sustainability. - Wealth Maximization (Value Maximization): - Principle: The modern, universally accepted objective asserting that the fundamental goal of the firm is to maximize the Net Present Worth of the enterprise, measured through the market value (stock price) of its equity shares: $$W = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0$$ where $CF_t$ is cash flow in year $t$, $k$ is discount rate (cost of capital), and $I_0$ is initial capital investment. - Superiority: Explicitly incorporates the Time Value of Money through discounted cash flows; fully accounts for project risk and uncertainty; considers the timing and sustainability of cash flows; aligns the interests of management with shareholders.

2. Long-Term and Short-Term Sources of Capital: - Long-Term Capital Sources: 1. Equity Shares (Common Stock): Represents permanent ownership capital. Equity holders possess voting rights and receive dividends from residual profits. Incurs zero mandatory fixed interest burden, but dilutes ownership and carries the highest cost of capital ($k_e$). 2. Preference Shares: Hybrid security holding preferential rights over equity shareholders regarding fixed annual dividend payout and asset distribution during liquidation, but typically without voting rights. 3. Debentures / Corporate Bonds: Long-term debt instruments carrying a legally binding fixed interest rate ($k_d$). Interest payments are tax-deductible expenses ($k_d(1 - T)$), lowering the effective cost of debt. However, excessive debt increases financial leverage risk and default risk. 4. Retained Earnings: Internal financing achieved by ploughing back enterprise net profits into expansion projects rather than distributing them as dividends. Eliminates flotation and underwriting costs. 5. Term Loans from Financial Institutions: Medium-to-long term secured debt provided by banks (IDBI, ICICI, SBI) with structured amortization schedules. 6. Venture Capital and Private Equity: Equity investments provided to early-stage, high-growth technology startups in exchange for significant equity ownership and board representation. - Short-Term Capital (Working Capital Sources): Commercial bank overdrafts, cash credit, trade credit from suppliers, factoring of accounts receivable, and short-term commercial paper.

3. Fundamental Financial Statements: - The Balance Sheet: A snapshot financial statement presenting the financial position of the enterprise at a specific point in time (e.g., as of March 31). It is strictly governed by the Fundamental Accounting Equation: $$\mathbf{\text{Total Assets} = \text{Total Liabilities} + \text{Shareholders' Equity}}$$ - Assets: Resources owned by the firm (Fixed Assets: plant, machinery; Current Assets: cash, inventory, accounts receivable). - Liabilities: Obligations owed to external creditors (Long-Term Debt, Current Liabilities: accounts payable, accrued expenses). - Equity: Residual interest belonging to owners (Paid-up share capital + Reserves and Surplus). - Profit and Loss Statement (Income Statement): A period financial statement summarizing operational revenues earned and expenses incurred over an accounting interval (e.g., April 1 to March 31): $$\text{Net Sales Revenue} - \text{Cost of Goods Sold (COGS)} = \text{Gross Profit}$$ $$\text{Gross Profit} - \text{Operating Expenses (Selling, Admin, R\&D)} = \text{EBIT (Operating Profit)}$$ $$\text{EBIT} - \text{Interest Expense} = \text{EBT (Earnings Before Taxes)}$$ $$\text{EBT} - \text{Corporate Taxes} = \text{EAT (Net Profit After Tax)}$$

Question 22 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: MGT-Q22

Q22. Derive the mathematical expressions for Break-Even Point (BEP in units and revenue), P/V Ratio, and Margin of Safety. Illustrate with a break-even chart and numerical example. Q22. Derive the mathematical expressions for Break-Even Point (BEP in units and revenue), P/V Ratio, and Margin of Safety. Illustrate with a break-even chart and numerical example. Q22. Derive the mathematical expressions for Break-Even Point (BEP in units and revenue), P/V Ratio, and Margin of Safety. Illustrate with a break-even chart and numerical example.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (HUT300) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 19(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P - v} = \frac{FC}{C}, \quad S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V \text{ Ratio}}, \quad MoS \% = \frac{S_{actual} - S_{BEP}}{S_{actual}} \times 100\%$$

Break-Even Point: Zero-profit sales volume where total revenue equals total costs ($FC + v \cdot Q$). Contribution margin $C = P - v$. Margin of Safety ($MoS$) measures cushion above BEP.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P - v} = \frac{FC}{C}, \quad S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V \text{ Ratio}}, \quad MoS \% = \frac{S_{actual} - S_{BEP}}{S_{actual}} \times 100\%$$

Break-Even Point: Zero-profit sales volume where total revenue equals total costs ($FC + v \cdot Q$). Contribution margin $C = P - v$. Margin of Safety ($MoS$) measures cushion above BEP.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P - v} = \frac{FC}{C}, \quad S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V \text{ Ratio}}, \quad MoS \% = \frac{S_{actual} - S_{BEP}}{S_{actual}} \times 100\%$$

Break-Even Point: Zero-profit sales volume where total revenue equals total costs ($FC + v \cdot Q$). Contribution margin $C = P - v$. Margin of Safety ($MoS$) measures cushion above BEP.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Break-Even Analysis (Cost-Volume-Profit or CVP Analysis) is an essential financial modeling tool used by engineering managers to determine the minimum sales volume and capacity utilization required for an enterprise to operate without financial loss.

1. Cost Classification in CVP Analysis: - Total Fixed Cost ($FC$): Expenses that remain constant in total amount regardless of production volume within the relevant range (e.g., factory rent, executive salaries, depreciation of plant, property insurance). - Variable Cost per Unit ($v$): Expenses that vary in direct, linear proportion to production volume (e.g., direct raw material, direct shop-floor labor, machine electricity consumption). Total Variable Cost is $TVC = v \cdot Q$. - Selling Price per Unit ($P$): Revenue earned per unit sold. Total Sales Revenue is $TR = P \cdot Q$. - Contribution Margin per Unit ($C$): The surplus revenue generated by each unit sold after recovering its direct variable cost, which contributes toward covering fixed overheads and generating operating profit: $$C = P - v$$

2. Mathematical Derivation of Break-Even Point: Total Cost ($TC$) of producing $Q$ units is: $$TC = FC + TVC = FC + v \cdot Q$$ Total Revenue ($TR$) from selling $Q$ units is: $$TR = P \cdot Q$$ Total Operating Profit ($\Pi$) is the difference between total revenue and total cost: $$\Pi = TR - TC = (P \cdot Q) - (FC + v \cdot Q) = (P - v)Q - FC$$

By fundamental definition, the Break-Even Point ($BEP$) is the unique level of production and sales volume at which total revenue equals total cost, resulting in exactly Zero Net Profit ($\Pi = 0$): $$(P - v) Q_{BEP} - FC = 0 \implies (P - v) Q_{BEP} = FC$$ Solving for physical sales volume in units: $$\mathbf{Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P - v} = \frac{FC}{\text{Unit Contribution Margin } (C)}} \quad \text{units}$$

To determine the Break-Even Sales Revenue ($S_{BEP}$ in monetary currency): $$S_{BEP} = Q_{BEP} \cdot P = \left( \frac{FC}{P - v} \right) P = \frac{FC}{\frac{P - v}{P}}$$ The denominator term $\frac{P - v}{P}$ is defined as the Profit-Volume Ratio ($P/V$ Ratio or Contribution-to-Sales Ratio): $$P/V \text{ Ratio} = \frac{P - v}{P} = \frac{\text{Contribution Margin}}{\text{Sales Price}} = \frac{\Delta \text{Profit}}{\Delta \text{Sales}}$$ Thus: $$\mathbf{S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V \text{ Ratio}}} \quad \text{INR}$$

3. Margin of Safety ($MoS$): The Margin of Safety represents the buffer or cushion by which actual or budgeted sales exceed the break-even sales volume. It indicates how much sales can fall before the company incurs an operating loss: $$MoS_{\text{units}} = Q_{\text{actual}} - Q_{BEP}$$ $$MoS_{\text{revenue}} = S_{\text{actual}} - S_{BEP}$$ Expressed as a percentage ratio: $$\mathbf{MoS \% = \frac{S_{\text{actual}} - S_{BEP}}{S_{\text{actual}}} \times 100\% = \frac{\text{Operating Profit}}{\text{Total Contribution Margin}} \times 100\%}$$

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical appliance manufacturer produces miniature circuit breakers (MCBs). The cost accounting department provides the following financial figures for the upcoming financial year: - Annual Total Fixed Overhead Costs ($FC$) = $\text{INR } 18,00,000$ - Direct raw material cost per MCB = $\text{INR } 45$ - Direct manufacturing labor cost per MCB = $\text{INR } 25$ - Variable sales commission and packaging per MCB = $\text{INR } 10$ - Selling price per unit ($P$) = $\text{INR } 160$ - Budgeted production and sales for the year = $30,000\text{ units}$ Calculate: (a) The unit variable cost ($v$), unit contribution margin ($C$), and the $P/V$ Ratio. (b) The Break-Even Point in physical units ($Q_{BEP}$) and sales turnover ($S_{BEP}$). (c) The Margin of Safety in sales revenue and as a percentage of budgeted sales. (d) The net profit earned at the budgeted volume of $30,000\text{ units}$. (e) The required sales volume to earn a target net profit of $\text{INR } 9,00,000$.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Compute unit variable cost and unit contribution margin: $$v = 45 + 25 + 10 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$C = P - v = 160 - 80 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$P/V \text{ Ratio} = \frac{C}{P} = \frac{80}{160} = 0.50 \implies 50\%$$

Step 2: Calculate Break-Even Point: $$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{C} = \frac{18,00,000}{80} = 22,500\text{ units}$$ $$S_{BEP} = Q_{BEP} \times P = 22,500 \times 160 = \text{INR } 36,00,000$$ (Alternatively: $S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V} = \frac{18,00,000}{0.50} = \text{INR } 36,00,000$).

Step 3: Calculate Margin of Safety at budgeted volume ($Q = 30,000$): Actual Budgeted Sales Turnover: $$S_{actual} = 30,000 \times 160 = \text{INR } 48,00,000$$ $$MoS_{\text{revenue}} = 48,00,000 - 36,00,000 = \text{INR } 12,00,000$$ $$MoS \% = \frac{12,00,000}{48,00,000} \times 100\% = 25.0\%$$

Step 4: Compute Net Profit at budgeted volume: $$\Pi = (Q \cdot C) - FC = (30,000 \times 80) - 18,00,000 = 24,00,000 - 18,00,000 = \text{INR } 6,00,000$$

Step 5: Compute required sales volume for target profit ($\Pi_{target} = \text{INR } 9,00,000$): $$Q_{target} = \frac{FC + \Pi_{target}}{C} = \frac{18,00,000 + 9,00,000}{80} = \frac{27,00,000}{80} = 33,750\text{ units}$$

Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{Q_{BEP} = 22,500\text{ units}}, \quad \mathbf{S_{BEP} = \text{INR } 36,00,000}, \quad \mathbf{MoS = 25\%}, \quad \mathbf{\Pi = \text{INR } 6,00,000}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical appliance manufacturer produces miniature circuit breakers (MCBs). (b) The Break-Even Point in physical units ($Q_{BEP}$) and sales turnover ($S_{BEP}$). (c) The Margin of Safety in sales revenue and as a percentage of budgeted sales. (d) The net profit earned at the budgeted volume of $30,000\text{ units}$. (e) The required sales volume to earn a target net profit of $\text{INR } 9,00,000$.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Compute unit variable cost and unit contribution margin: $$v = 45 + 25 + 10 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$C = P - v = 160 - 80 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$P/V \text{ Ratio} = \frac{C}{P} = \frac{80}{160} = 0.50 \implies 50\%$$

Step 2: Calculate Break-Even Point: $$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{C} = \frac{18,00,000}{80} = 22,500\text{ units}$$ $$S_{BEP} = Q_{BEP} \times P = 22,500 \times 160 = \text{INR } 36,00,000$$ (Alternatively: $S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V} = \frac{18,00,000}{0.50} = \text{INR } 36,00,000$).

Step 4: Compute Net Profit at budgeted volume: $$\Pi = (Q \cdot C) - FC = (30,000 \times 80) - 18,00,000 = 24,00,000 - 18,00,000 = \text{INR } 6,00,000$$

Step 5: Compute required sales volume for target profit ($\Pi_{target} = \text{INR } 9,00,000$): $$Q_{target} = \frac{FC + \Pi_{target}}{C} = \frac{18,00,000 + 9,00,000}{80} = \frac{27,00,000}{80} = 33,750\text{ units}$$

Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{Q_{BEP} = 22,500\text{ units}}, \quad \mathbf{S_{BEP} = \text{INR } 36,00,000}, \quad \mathbf{MoS = 25\%}, \quad \mathbf{\Pi = \text{INR } 6,00,000}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Break-Even Analysis (Cost-Volume-Profit or CVP Analysis) is an essential financial modeling tool used by engineering managers to determine the minimum sales volume and capacity utilization required for an enterprise to operate without financial loss.

1. Cost Classification in CVP Analysis: - Total Fixed Cost ($FC$): Expenses that remain constant in total amount regardless of production volume within the relevant range (e.g., factory rent, executive salaries, depreciation of plant, property insurance). - Variable Cost per Unit ($v$): Expenses that vary in direct, linear proportion to production volume (e.g., direct raw material, direct shop-floor labor, machine electricity consumption). Total Variable Cost is $TVC = v \cdot Q$. - Selling Price per Unit ($P$): Revenue earned per unit sold. Total Sales Revenue is $TR = P \cdot Q$. - Contribution Margin per Unit ($C$): The surplus revenue generated by each unit sold after recovering its direct variable cost, which contributes toward covering fixed overheads and generating operating profit: $$C = P - v$$

2. Mathematical Derivation of Break-Even Point: Total Cost ($TC$) of producing $Q$ units is: $$TC = FC + TVC = FC + v \cdot Q$$ Total Revenue ($TR$) from selling $Q$ units is: $$TR = P \cdot Q$$ Total Operating Profit ($\Pi$) is the difference between total revenue and total cost: $$\Pi = TR - TC = (P \cdot Q) - (FC + v \cdot Q) = (P - v)Q - FC$$

By fundamental definition, the Break-Even Point ($BEP$) is the unique level of production and sales volume at which total revenue equals total cost, resulting in exactly Zero Net Profit ($\Pi = 0$): $$(P - v) Q_{BEP} - FC = 0 \implies (P - v) Q_{BEP} = FC$$ Solving for physical sales volume in units: $$\mathbf{Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P - v} = \frac{FC}{\text{Unit Contribution Margin } (C)}} \quad \text{units}$$

To determine the Break-Even Sales Revenue ($S_{BEP}$ in monetary currency): $$S_{BEP} = Q_{BEP} \cdot P = \left( \frac{FC}{P - v} \right) P = \frac{FC}{\frac{P - v}{P}}$$ The denominator term $\frac{P - v}{P}$ is defined as the Profit-Volume Ratio ($P/V$ Ratio or Contribution-to-Sales Ratio): $$P/V \text{ Ratio} = \frac{P - v}{P} = \frac{\text{Contribution Margin}}{\text{Sales Price}} = \frac{\Delta \text{Profit}}{\Delta \text{Sales}}$$ Thus: $$\mathbf{S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V \text{ Ratio}}} \quad \text{INR}$$

3. Margin of Safety ($MoS$): The Margin of Safety represents the buffer or cushion by which actual or budgeted sales exceed the break-even sales volume. It indicates how much sales can fall before the company incurs an operating loss: $$MoS_{\text{units}} = Q_{\text{actual}} - Q_{BEP}$$ $$MoS_{\text{revenue}} = S_{\text{actual}} - S_{BEP}$$ Expressed as a percentage ratio: $$\mathbf{MoS \% = \frac{S_{\text{actual}} - S_{BEP}}{S_{\text{actual}}} \times 100\% = \frac{\text{Operating Profit}}{\text{Total Contribution Margin}} \times 100\%}$$

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical appliance manufacturer produces miniature circuit breakers (MCBs). The cost accounting department provides the following financial figures for the upcoming financial year: - Annual Total Fixed Overhead Costs ($FC$) = $\text{INR } 18,00,000$ - Direct raw material cost per MCB = $\text{INR } 45$ - Direct manufacturing labor cost per MCB = $\text{INR } 25$ - Variable sales commission and packaging per MCB = $\text{INR } 10$ - Selling price per unit ($P$) = $\text{INR } 160$ - Budgeted production and sales for the year = $30,000\text{ units}$ Calculate: (a) The unit variable cost ($v$), unit contribution margin ($C$), and the $P/V$ Ratio. (b) The Break-Even Point in physical units ($Q_{BEP}$) and sales turnover ($S_{BEP}$). (c) The Margin of Safety in sales revenue and as a percentage of budgeted sales. (d) The net profit earned at the budgeted volume of $30,000\text{ units}$. (e) The required sales volume to earn a target net profit of $\text{INR } 9,00,000$.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Step 1: Compute unit variable cost and unit contribution margin: $$v = 45 + 25 + 10 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$C = P - v = 160 - 80 = \text{INR } 80\text{ per unit}$$ $$P/V \text{ Ratio} = \frac{C}{P} = \frac{80}{160} = 0.50 \implies 50\%$$

Step 2: Calculate Break-Even Point: $$Q_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{C} = \frac{18,00,000}{80} = 22,500\text{ units}$$ $$S_{BEP} = Q_{BEP} \times P = 22,500 \times 160 = \text{INR } 36,00,000$$ (Alternatively: $S_{BEP} = \frac{FC}{P/V} = \frac{18,00,000}{0.50} = \text{INR } 36,00,000$).

Step 3: Calculate Margin of Safety at budgeted volume ($Q = 30,000$): Actual Budgeted Sales Turnover: $$S_{actual} = 30,000 \times 160 = \text{INR } 48,00,000$$ $$MoS_{\text{revenue}} = 48,00,000 - 36,00,000 = \text{INR } 12,00,000$$ $$MoS \% = \frac{12,00,000}{48,00,000} \times 100\% = 25.0\%$$

Step 4: Compute Net Profit at budgeted volume: $$\Pi = (Q \cdot C) - FC = (30,000 \times 80) - 18,00,000 = 24,00,000 - 18,00,000 = \text{INR } 6,00,000$$

Step 5: Compute required sales volume for target profit ($\Pi_{target} = \text{INR } 9,00,000$): $$Q_{target} = \frac{FC + \Pi_{target}}{C} = \frac{18,00,000 + 9,00,000}{80} = \frac{27,00,000}{80} = 33,750\text{ units}$$

Final highlighted results: $$\mathbf{Q_{BEP} = 22,500\text{ units}}, \quad \mathbf{S_{BEP} = \text{INR } 36,00,000}, \quad \mathbf{MoS = 25\%}, \quad \mathbf{\Pi = \text{INR } 6,00,000}$$

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.2: Cost-Volume-Profit Break-Even Chart Illustrating Fixed Costs, Variable Costs, and Margin of Safety — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
Question 23 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Numerical Problem ID: MGT-Q23

Q23. Explain capital budgeting evaluation techniques: Payback Period, ARR, NPV, IRR, and Profitability Index. Compare two mutually exclusive projects using a step-by-step numerical. Q23. Explain capital budgeting evaluation techniques: Payback Period, ARR, NPV, IRR, and Profitability Index. Compare two mutually exclusive projects using a step-by-step numerical. Q23. Explain capital budgeting evaluation techniques: Payback Period, ARR, NPV, IRR, and Profitability Index. Compare two mutually exclusive projects using a step-by-step numerical.

$$NPV = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0 > 0, \quad \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + IRR)^t} - I_0 = 0, \quad PI = \frac{PV}{I_0}$$

$$NPV = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0 > 0, \quad \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + IRR)^t} - I_0 = 0, \quad PI = \frac{PV}{I_0}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$NPV = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0 > 0, \quad \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + IRR)^t} - I_0 = 0, \quad PI = \frac{PV}{I_0}$$

Capital budgeting evaluates long-term investments. Non-discounted Payback Period ignores post-payback cash flows. Modern DCF criteria prioritize Net Present Value ($NPV$), Internal Rate of Return ($IRR$), and Profitability Index ($PI$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Capital budgeting is the corporate decision-making process for evaluating, selecting, and allocating capital funds to major long-term engineering investment projects (such as plant expansions, new automated lines, or robotic automation) whose cash flows extend beyond one year.

1. Classical Evaluation Methods: - Traditional (Non-Discounted) Techniques: 1. Payback Period ($PBP$): The exact time required for the cumulative cash inflows from an investment project to fully recover the initial capital outlay ($I_0$). Ignores cash flows occurring after the payback cutoff and ignores the time value of money. 2. Accounting Rate of Return ($ARR$): $$ARR = \frac{\text{Average Annual Accounting Net Profit After Taxes}}{\text{Average Investment}} \times 100\% = \frac{\bar{\Pi}_{net}}{(I_0 + S)/2} \times 100\%$$ where $S$ is scrap/salvage value. Uses accounting profits rather than cash flows and ignores discounting.

- Modern Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) Techniques: 1. Net Present Value ($NPV$): The gold-standard criterion. Evaluates the difference between the present value of all future operating cash inflows discounted at the firm's cost of capital ($k$) and the initial investment outlay: $$\mathbf{NPV = \sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t} - I_0}$$ Decision Rule: Accept project if $NPV > 0$; reject if $NPV < 0$. Between mutually exclusive projects, select the project with the highest positive $NPV$. 2. Internal Rate of Return ($IRR$): The intrinsic discount rate ($r$) that equates the present value of expected future cash inflows exactly to the initial investment outlay, rendering $NPV = 0$: $$\sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + IRR)^t} - I_0 = 0$$ Decision Rule: Accept if $IRR > k$ (hurdle cost of capital); reject if $IRR < k$. 3. Profitability Index ($PI$ / Benefit-Cost Ratio): $$PI = \frac{\sum_{t=1}^n \frac{CF_t}{(1 + k)^t}}{I_0}$$ Accept if $PI > 1.0$.

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical switchgear manufacturing company is evaluating two mutually exclusive automated CNC machinery proposals: Project A and Project B. Both require an initial capital outlay of $\text{INR } 5,00,000$. The company's cost of capital (discount rate) is $10\%$. Cash inflows are after tax but before depreciation. Estimated cash flows are:

| Year | Cash Inflow Project A ($\text{INR}$) | Cash Inflow Project B ($\text{INR}$) | Present Value Factor ($PVF$ at $10\%$) | |---|---|---|---| | 1 | $2,00,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.909$ | | 2 | $2,00,000$ | $1,50,000$ | $0.826$ | | 3 | $1,50,000$ | $2,00,000$ | $0.751$ | | 4 | $1,00,000$ | $2,50,000$ | $0.683$ | | 5 | $50,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.621$ |

Evaluate both proposals using: (a) Payback Period ($PBP$). (b) Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$. (c) Profitability Index ($PI$). Recommend which project management should select, stating justifications.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Part (a): Payback Period ($PBP$): - For Project A ($I_0 = 5,00,000$): - Year 1: Cumulative cash flow = $\text{INR } 2,00,000$ - Year 2: Cumulative cash flow = $2,00,000 + 2,00,000 = \text{INR } 4,00,000$ - Year 3 inflow is $1,50,000$. Unrecovered balance after Year 2 = $5,00,000 - 4,00,000 = 1,00,000$. - Fraction of Year 3 required = $\frac{1,00,000}{1,50,000} = 0.67\text{ years}$. - $PBP_A = 2 + 0.67 = \mathbf{2.67\text{ years}}$ (2 years, 8 months).

- For Project B ($I_0 = 5,00,000$): - Year 1: $\text{INR } 1,00,000$ - Year 2: $1,00,000 + 1,50,000 = \text{INR } 2,50,000$ - Year 3: $2,50,000 + 2,00,000 = \text{INR } 4,50,000$ - Year 4 inflow is $2,50,000$. Unrecovered balance after Year 3 = $5,00,000 - 4,50,000 = 50,000$. - Fraction of Year 4 required = $\frac{50,000}{2,50,000} = 0.20\text{ years}$. - $PBP_B = 3 + 0.20 = \mathbf{3.20\text{ years}}$ (3 years, 2.4 months).

Part (b): Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$: - For Project A: $$PV_1 = 2,00,000 \times 0.909 = 1,81,800$$ $$PV_2 = 2,00,000 \times 0.826 = 1,65,200$$ $$PV_3 = 1,50,000 \times 0.751 = 1,12,650$$ $$PV_4 = 1,00,000 \times 0.683 = 68,300$$ $$PV_5 = 50,000 \times 0.621 = 31,050$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_A) = 1,81,800 + 1,65,200 + 1,12,650 + 68,300 + 31,050 = \text{INR } 5,59,000$$ $$NPV_A = PV_A - I_0 = 5,59,000 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 59,000}$$

- For Project B: $$PV_1 = 1,00,000 \times 0.909 = 90,900$$ $$PV_2 = 1,50,000 \times 0.826 = 1,23,900$$ $$PV_3 = 2,00,000 \times 0.751 = 1,50,200$$ $$PV_4 = 2,50,000 \times 0.683 = 1,70,750$$ $$PV_5 = 1,00,000 \times 0.621 = 62,100$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_B) = 90,900 + 1,23,900 + 1,50,200 + 1,70,750 + 62,100 = \text{INR } 5,97,850$$ $$NPV_B = PV_B - I_0 = 5,97,850 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 97,850}$$

Part (c): Profitability Index ($PI$): $$PI_A = \frac{PV_A}{I_0} = \frac{5,59,000}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.118}$$ $$PI_B = \frac{PV_B}{I_0} = \frac{5,97,850}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.196}$$

RECOMMENDATION AND JUSTIFICATION: Although Project A has a shorter Payback Period ($2.67\text{ years}$ vs $3.20\text{ years}$), Payback Period is an unreliable criterion because it completely ignores subsequent cash flows and the time value of money. Under modern discounted cash flow principles, **Project B must be selected** because it yields a significantly higher Net Present Value ($NPV_B = \text{INR } 97,850 > NPV_A = \text{INR } 59,000$) and a superior Profitability Index ($PI_B = 1.196 > PI_A = 1.118$), thereby adding greater total net wealth to the enterprise.

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical switchgear manufacturing company is evaluating two mutually exclusive automated CNC machinery proposals: Project A and Project B. Both require an initial capital outlay of $\text{INR } 5,00,000$. The company's cost of capital (discount rate) is $10\%$. Cash inflows are after tax but before depreciation. Estimated cash flows are:

| Year | Cash Inflow Project A ($\text{INR}$) | Cash Inflow Project B ($\text{INR}$) | Present Value Factor ($PVF$ at $10\%$) | |---|---|---|---| | 1 | $2,00,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.909$ | | 2 | $2,00,000$ | $1,50,000$ | $0.826$ | | 3 | $1,50,000$ | $2,00,000$ | $0.751$ | | 4 | $1,00,000$ | $2,50,000$ | $0.683$ | | 5 | $50,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.621$ |

Evaluate both proposals using: (a) Payback Period ($PBP$). (b) Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$. (c) Profitability Index ($PI$). Recommend which project management should select, stating justifications.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Part (a): Payback Period ($PBP$): - For Project A ($I_0 = 5,00,000$): - Year 1: Cumulative cash flow = $\text{INR } 2,00,000$ - Year 2: Cumulative cash flow = $2,00,000 + 2,00,000 = \text{INR } 4,00,000$ - Year 3 inflow is $1,50,000$. Unrecovered balance after Year 2 = $5,00,000 - 4,00,000 = 1,00,000$. - Fraction of Year 3 required = $\frac{1,00,000}{1,50,000} = 0.67\text{ years}$. - $PBP_A = 2 + 0.67 = \mathbf{2.67\text{ years}}$ (2 years, 8 months).

- For Project B ($I_0 = 5,00,000$): - Year 1: $\text{INR } 1,00,000$ - Year 2: $1,00,000 + 1,50,000 = \text{INR } 2,50,000$ - Year 3: $2,50,000 + 2,00,000 = \text{INR } 4,50,000$ - Year 4 inflow is $2,50,000$. Unrecovered balance after Year 3 = $5,00,000 - 4,50,000 = 50,000$. - Fraction of Year 4 required = $\frac{50,000}{2,50,000} = 0.20\text{ years}$. - $PBP_B = 3 + 0.20 = \mathbf{3.20\text{ years}}$ (3 years, 2.4 months).

Part (b): Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$: - For Project A: $$PV_1 = 2,00,000 \times 0.909 = 1,81,800$$ $$PV_2 = 2,00,000 \times 0.826 = 1,65,200$$ $$PV_3 = 1,50,000 \times 0.751 = 1,12,650$$ $$PV_4 = 1,00,000 \times 0.683 = 68,300$$ $$PV_5 = 50,000 \times 0.621 = 31,050$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_A) = 1,81,800 + 1,65,200 + 1,12,650 + 68,300 + 31,050 = \text{INR } 5,59,000$$ $$NPV_A = PV_A - I_0 = 5,59,000 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 59,000}$$

- For Project B: $$PV_1 = 1,00,000 \times 0.909 = 90,900$$ $$PV_2 = 1,50,000 \times 0.826 = 1,23,900$$ $$PV_3 = 2,00,000 \times 0.751 = 1,50,200$$ $$PV_4 = 2,50,000 \times 0.683 = 1,70,750$$ $$PV_5 = 1,00,000 \times 0.621 = 62,100$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_B) = 90,900 + 1,23,900 + 1,50,200 + 1,70,750 + 62,100 = \text{INR } 5,97,850$$ $$NPV_B = PV_B - I_0 = 5,97,850 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 97,850}$$

Part (c): Profitability Index ($PI$): $$PI_A = \frac{PV_A}{I_0} = \frac{5,59,000}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.118}$$ $$PI_B = \frac{PV_B}{I_0} = \frac{5,97,850}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.196}$$

RECOMMENDATION AND JUSTIFICATION: Although Project A has a shorter Payback Period ($2.67\text{ years}$ vs $3.20\text{ years}$), Payback Period is an unreliable criterion because it completely ignores subsequent cash flows and the time value of money. Under modern discounted cash flow principles, **Project B must be selected** because it yields a significantly higher Net Present Value ($NPV_B = \text{INR } 97,850 > NPV_A = \text{INR } 59,000$) and a superior Profitability Index ($PI_B = 1.196 > PI_A = 1.118$), thereby adding greater total net wealth to the enterprise.

--- NUMERICAL PROBLEM (KTU Examination Model): An electrical switchgear manufacturing company is evaluating two mutually exclusive automated CNC machinery proposals: Project A and Project B. Both require an initial capital outlay of $\text{INR } 5,00,000$. The company's cost of capital (discount rate) is $10\%$. Cash inflows are after tax but before depreciation. Estimated cash flows are:

| Year | Cash Inflow Project A ($\text{INR}$) | Cash Inflow Project B ($\text{INR}$) | Present Value Factor ($PVF$ at $10\%$) | |---|---|---|---| | 1 | $2,00,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.909$ | | 2 | $2,00,000$ | $1,50,000$ | $0.826$ | | 3 | $1,50,000$ | $2,00,000$ | $0.751$ | | 4 | $1,00,000$ | $2,50,000$ | $0.683$ | | 5 | $50,000$ | $1,00,000$ | $0.621$ |

Evaluate both proposals using: (a) Payback Period ($PBP$). (b) Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$. (c) Profitability Index ($PI$). Recommend which project management should select, stating justifications.

SOLUTION STEP-BY-STEP: Part (a): Payback Period ($PBP$): - For Project A ($I_0 = 5,00,000$): - Year 1: Cumulative cash flow = $\text{INR } 2,00,000$ - Year 2: Cumulative cash flow = $2,00,000 + 2,00,000 = \text{INR } 4,00,000$ - Year 3 inflow is $1,50,000$. Unrecovered balance after Year 3 = $5,00,000 - 4,50,000 = 50,000$. - Fraction of Year 4 required = $\frac{50,000}{2,50,000} = 0.20\text{ years}$. - $PBP_B = 3 + 0.20 = \mathbf{3.20\text{ years}}$ (3 years, 2.4 months).

Part (b): Net Present Value ($NPV$) at $10\%$: - For Project A: $$PV_1 = 2,00,000 \times 0.909 = 1,81,800$$ $$PV_2 = 2,00,000 \times 0.826 = 1,65,200$$ $$PV_3 = 1,50,000 \times 0.751 = 1,12,650$$ $$PV_4 = 1,00,000 \times 0.683 = 68,300$$ $$PV_5 = 50,000 \times 0.621 = 31,050$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_A) = 1,81,800 + 1,65,200 + 1,12,650 + 68,300 + 31,050 = \text{INR } 5,59,000$$ $$NPV_A = PV_A - I_0 = 5,59,000 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 59,000}$$

- For Project B: $$PV_1 = 1,00,000 \times 0.909 = 90,900$$ $$PV_2 = 1,50,000 \times 0.826 = 1,23,900$$ $$PV_3 = 2,00,000 \times 0.751 = 1,50,200$$ $$PV_4 = 2,50,000 \times 0.683 = 1,70,750$$ $$PV_5 = 1,00,000 \times 0.621 = 62,100$$ $$\text{Total Present Value } (PV_B) = 90,900 + 1,23,900 + 1,50,200 + 1,70,750 + 62,100 = \text{INR } 5,97,850$$ $$NPV_B = PV_B - I_0 = 5,97,850 - 5,00,000 = \mathbf{+\text{INR } 97,850}$$

Part (c): Profitability Index ($PI$): $$PI_A = \frac{PV_A}{I_0} = \frac{5,59,000}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.118}$$ $$PI_B = \frac{PV_B}{I_0} = \frac{5,97,850}{5,00,000} = \mathbf{1.196}$$

RECOMMENDATION AND JUSTIFICATION: Although Project A has a shorter Payback Period ($2.67\text{ years}$ vs $3.20\text{ years}$), Payback Period is an unreliable criterion because it completely ignores subsequent cash flows and the time value of money. Under modern discounted cash flow principles, **Project B must be selected** because it yields a significantly higher Net Present Value ($NPV_B = \text{INR } 97,850 > NPV_A = \text{INR } 59,000$) and a superior Profitability Index ($PI_B = 1.196 > PI_A = 1.118$), thereby adding greater total net wealth to the enterprise.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.3: Decision Trees for Capital Budgeting Methods and NPV Profile vs Discount Rate — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Q24. Explain project network construction rules in CPM. Detail the forward pass, backward pass, float calculations (total, free, independent), and identification of the critical path. Q24. Explain project network construction rules in CPM. Detail the forward pass, backward pass, float calculations (total, free, independent), and identification of the critical path. Q24. Explain project network construction rules in CPM. Detail the forward pass, backward pass, float calculations (total, free, independent), and identification of the critical path.

🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (HUT300) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 20(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$E_j = \max[E_i + t_{ij}], \quad L_i = \min[L_j - t_{ij}], \quad TF = (L_j - E_i) - t_{ij} = 0 \ (\text{Critical Path})$$

CPM Network Analysis: Forward pass finds earliest times ($E_j$); backward pass finds latest times ($L_i$). The Critical Path is the longest path through the network, defined by activities with Zero Total Float ($TF = 0$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$E_j = \max[E_i + t_{ij}], \quad L_i = \min[L_j - t_{ij}], \quad TF = (L_j - E_i) - t_{ij} = 0 \ (\text{Critical Path})$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Project management is the application of knowledge, skills, tools, and techniques to project activities to meet project requirements within defined time, cost, and quality specifications. Complex engineering projects (such as substation construction or new power plant commissioning) involve thousands of interdependent activities analyzed via Network Analysis techniques: the Critical Path Method (CPM) and the Program Evaluation and Review Technique (PERT).

1. Rules for Network Construction: 1. An activity is a distinct, time-consuming task represented by a directed arrow ($\to$). 2. An event is a point in time representing the commencement or completion of one or more activities, depicted as a circle or node ($\bigcirc$). An event consumes zero time and zero resources. 3. Every network must have exactly one starting node (initial event) and exactly one terminal node (end event). 4. Time flows unidirectionally from left to right; no looping or cyclic recurrence is permitted. 5. No two activities can share the exact same start event and same finish event. If two parallel activities have identical start and end nodes, a Dummy Activity (represented by a dashed arrow carrying zero duration and zero cost) must be inserted to preserve unique logical identification. 6. Dangling (an activity ending without connecting to the network) is strictly prohibited. 7. Fulkerson's Rule for Node Numbering: Start event is labeled 1. Cross out all arrows originating from node 1. Find all newly unentered nodes and number them 2, 3, etc., ensuring that for every activity $(i \to j)$, the start event number is strictly smaller than the finish event number ($i < j$).

2. Algorithmic Determination of the Critical Path: - Forward Pass Computation (Determining Earliest Times): We set the Earliest Start Time of the initial event 1 to zero: $E_1 = 0$. Moving progressively from left to right through all nodes $j$: $$\mathbf{E_j = \max_{i} [E_i + t_{ij}]}$$ where $t_{ij}$ is the duration of activity $(i \to j)$ and the maximum is taken over all incoming activities into node $j$. For an activity $(i \to j)$: - Earliest Start Time: $ES_{ij} = E_i$ - Earliest Finish Time: $EF_{ij} = ES_{ij} + t_{ij} = E_i + t_{ij}$

- Backward Pass Computation (Determining Latest Allowable Times): Let $n$ be the terminal event node. The latest allowable completion time equals its earliest completion time: $L_n = E_n$. Moving backward from right to left through all nodes $i$: $$\mathbf{L_i = \min_{j} [L_j - t_{ij}]}$$ where the minimum is taken over all activities emerging from node $i$. For an activity $(i \to j)$: - Latest Finish Time: $LF_{ij} = L_j$ - Latest Start Time: $LS_{ij} = LF_{ij} - t_{ij} = L_j - t_{ij}$

3. Mathematical Formulation of Floats (Slacks): Float is the measure of scheduling flexibility; it is the amount of time an activity can be delayed without extending the overall project completion date: 1. Total Float ($TF_{ij}$): The maximum time an activity can be delayed without delaying the total project completion time: $$TF_{ij} = LS_{ij} - ES_{ij} = LF_{ij} - EF_{ij} = (L_j - E_i) - t_{ij}$$ 2. Free Float ($FF_{ij}$): The portion of total float that can be utilized without delaying the earliest start time of any immediate successor activity: $$FF_{ij} = E_j - EF_{ij} = E_j - E_i - t_{ij} = TF_{ij} - (L_j - E_j)$$ 3. Independent Float ($IF_{ij}$): The time leeway available when the preceding activity finishes at its latest allowable time and the successor starts at its earliest time (guaranteed float): $$IF_{ij} = \max[0, \, (E_j - L_i - t_{ij})] = FF_{ij} - (L_i - E_i)$$

4. The Critical Path: The Critical Path is the continuous chain of sequential activities connecting the start event to the terminal event that possesses Zero Total Float ($TF_{ij} = 0$): $$E_i = L_i, \quad E_j = L_j, \quad E_j - E_i = t_{ij}$$ The Critical Path represents the longest path in terms of total duration through the network. Any delay along a critical activity directly and identically delays the final completion date of the entire project.

Q25. Explain the PERT technique. Derive the expected activity duration and variance using the Beta distribution, and determine the probability of project completion within target date. Q25. Explain the PERT technique. Derive the expected activity duration and variance using the Beta distribution, and determine the probability of project completion within target date. Q25. Explain the PERT technique. Derive the expected activity duration and variance using the Beta distribution, and determine the probability of project completion within target date.

KTU Exam Source: December 2023 University Exam (HUT300) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 20(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$t_e = \frac{t_o + 4 t_m + t_p}{6}, \quad \sigma^2 = \left(\frac{t_p - t_o}{6}\right)^2, \quad Z = \frac{T_s - T_e}{\sigma_p}$$

PERT Probabilistic Analysis: Three time estimates ($t_o, t_m, t_p$) follow a Beta distribution. Total project variance is the sum of critical path variances. Standard normal $Z$-score gives the probability of meeting target date $T_s$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$t_e = \frac{t_o + 4 t_m + t_p}{6}, \quad \sigma^2 = \left(\frac{t_p - t_o}{6}\right)^2, \quad Z = \frac{T_s - T_e}{\sigma_p}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$t_e = \frac{t_o + 4 t_m + t_p}{6}, \quad \sigma^2 = \left(\frac{t_p - t_o}{6}\right)^2, \quad Z = \frac{T_s - T_e}{\sigma_p}$$

PERT Probabilistic Analysis: Three time estimates ($t_o, t_m, t_p$) follow a Beta distribution. Total project variance is the sum of critical path variances. Standard normal $Z$-score gives the probability of meeting target date $T_s$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Program Evaluation and Review Technique (PERT) was developed in 1958 by the U.S. Navy Special Projects Office with Booz Allen Hamilton for the Polaris missile project. While CPM is deterministic (applied to repetitive construction where task times are known with high certainty), PERT is probabilistic, designed specifically for non-repetitive, R&D, and aerospace projects characterized by high technological uncertainty.

1. PERT Three Time Estimates: To account for uncertainty, PERT collects three independent time estimates for each activity $(i \to j)$: 1. Optimistic Time ($t_o$ or $a$): The minimum possible duration if everything proceeds exceptionally well, weather is ideal, and zero breakdowns occur (probability $< 1\%$). 2. Most Likely Time ($t_m$ or $m$): The modal duration occurring most frequently under normal, realistic operating conditions (the peak mode of the distribution). 3. Pessimistic Time ($t_p$ or $b$): The maximum possible duration if adverse complications occur, severe supply delays arise, or major technical hurdles emerge, excluding catastrophic acts of nature (probability $< 1\%$).

2. Derivation of Expected Duration and Variance using Beta Distribution: PERT models activity duration as following a continuous, unimodal Beta Distribution bounded within $[t_o, t_p]$. The beta distribution is chosen because it allows for skewness (non-symmetric distributions reflecting engineering realities). Assuming the total range $(t_p - t_o)$ represents approximately six standard deviations ($6\sigma$) of the distribution: $$6\sigma = t_p - t_o \implies \mathbf{\sigma = \frac{t_p - t_o}{6}}$$ The variance ($\sigma^2$) of the activity duration is: $$\mathbf{\sigma^2 = \left( \frac{t_p - t_o}{6} \right)^2}$$

The Expected Mean Duration ($t_e$ or $\mu$) of the activity is derived by taking a weighted polynomial approximation assigning a weight of 4 to the mode $t_m$ and 1 to each extreme tail ($t_o$ and $t_p$): $$\mathbf{t_e = \frac{t_o + 4 t_m + t_p}{6}}$$

3. Project Variance and Central Limit Theorem: Once $t_e$ is computed for all activities, standard CPM forward and backward pass algorithms identify the Critical Path. Let $CP$ denote the set of all activities lying on the critical path. The Expected Total Project Duration ($T_e$) is the sum of expected durations of critical activities: $$T_e = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} t_{e, ij}$$ By the Central Limit Theorem, although individual activity durations follow skewed Beta distributions, the total project duration distribution approaches a Gaussian Normal Distribution $\mathcal{N}(T_e, \sigma_p^2)$ because it is the sum of multiple independent random variables. The Total Project Variance ($\sigma_p^2$) is the sum of the variances of only the critical path activities: $$\mathbf{\sigma_p^2 = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \sigma_{ij}^2 = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \left( \frac{t_{p, ij} - t_{o, ij}}{6} \right)^2}$$ The standard deviation of project duration is: $$\mathbf{\sigma_p = \sqrt{\sigma_p^2} = \sqrt{\sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \sigma_{ij}^2}}$$ Note: Non-critical activities do not contribute to project completion variance unless their float is small enough that high variance could create a secondary critical path.

4. Probability of Completing Project Within Target Schedule ($T_s$): Let $T_s$ be a contractually mandated target completion date. We normalize the target date into a standard normal variable $Z$ with mean $\mu = 0$ and variance $\sigma = 1$: $$\mathbf{Z = \frac{T_s - T_e}{\sigma_p}}$$ The probability of project completion on or before target date $T_s$ is given by the cumulative standard normal distribution function: $$P(T \le T_s) = \Phi(Z) = \int_{-\infty}^Z \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{-u^2/2} \, du$$ From standard normal probability tables: - If $T_s = T_e \implies Z = 0 \implies P = 50\%$. - If $Z = +1.00 \implies P = 84.13\%$. - If $Z = +1.645 \implies P = 95.0\%$. - If $Z = +2.00 \implies P = 97.72\%$. - If $Z = -1.00 \implies P = 15.87\%$.

Assuming the total range $(t_p - t_o)$ represents approximately six standard deviations ($6\sigma$) of the distribution: $$6\sigma = t_p - t_o \implies \mathbf{\sigma = \frac{t_p - t_o}{6}}$$ The variance ($\sigma^2$) of the activity duration is: $$\mathbf{\sigma^2 = \left( \frac{t_p - t_o}{6} \right)^2}$$

The Expected Mean Duration ($t_e$ or $\mu$) of the activity is derived by taking a weighted polynomial approximation assigning a weight of 4 to the mode $t_m$ and 1 to each extreme tail ($t_o$ and $t_p$): $$\mathbf{t_e = \frac{t_o + 4 t_m + t_p}{6}}$$

3. Project Variance and Central Limit Theorem: Once $t_e$ is computed for all activities, standard CPM forward and backward pass algorithms identify the Critical Path. Let $CP$ denote the set of all activities lying on the critical path. The Expected Total Project Duration ($T_e$) is the sum of expected durations of critical activities: $$T_e = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} t_{e, ij}$$ By the Central Limit Theorem, although individual activity durations follow skewed Beta distributions, the total project duration distribution approaches a Gaussian Normal Distribution $\mathcal{N}(T_e, \sigma_p^2)$ because it is the sum of multiple independent random variables. The Total Project Variance ($\sigma_p^2$) is the sum of the variances of only the critical path activities: $$\mathbf{\sigma_p^2 = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \sigma_{ij}^2 = \sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \left( \frac{t_{p, ij} - t_{o, ij}}{6} \right)^2}$$ The standard deviation of project duration is: $$\mathbf{\sigma_p = \sqrt{\sigma_p^2} = \sqrt{\sum_{(i,j) \in CP} \sigma_{ij}^2}}$$ Note: Non-critical activities do not contribute to project completion variance unless their float is small enough that high variance could create a secondary critical path.

4. Probability of Completing Project Within Target Schedule ($T_s$): Let $T_s$ be a contractually mandated target completion date. We normalize the target date into a standard normal variable $Z$ with mean $\mu = 0$ and variance $\sigma = 1$: $$\mathbf{Z = \frac{T_s - T_e}{\sigma_p}}$$ The probability of project completion on or before target date $T_s$ is given by the cumulative standard normal distribution function: $$P(T \le T_s) = \Phi(Z) = \int_{-\infty}^Z \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{-u^2/2} \, du$$ From standard normal probability tables: - If $T_s = T_e \implies Z = 0 \implies P = 50\%$. - If $Z = +1.00 \implies P = 84.13\%$. - If $Z = +1.645 \implies P = 95.0\%$. - If $Z = +2.00 \implies P = 97.72\%$. - If $Z = -1.00 \implies P = 15.87\%$.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.5: PERT Beta Probability Distribution and Standard Normal Bell Curve for Target Date — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
Question 26 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: MGT-Q26

Q26. Explain the concept of project crashing and time-cost trade-offs. Derive the cost slope formula and outline the step-by-step algorithm to optimize total project cost. Q26. Explain the concept of project crashing and time-cost trade-offs. Derive the cost slope formula and outline the step-by-step algorithm to optimize total project cost. Q26. Explain the concept of project crashing and time-cost trade-offs. Derive the cost slope formula and outline the step-by-step algorithm to optimize total project cost.

KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (HUT300) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 20(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Cost Slope} = \frac{CC - NC}{NT - CT}, \quad \text{Total Cost} = \text{Direct Cost} + \text{Indirect Cost}$$

Project Crashing: Expedites critical activities having the lowest cost slope. Compressing project duration increases direct costs but decreases indirect overhead costs, optimizing total project cost.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{Cost Slope} = \frac{CC - NC}{NT - CT}, \quad \text{Total Cost} = \text{Direct Cost} + \text{Indirect Cost}$$

Project Crashing: Expedites critical activities having the lowest cost slope. Compressing project duration increases direct costs but decreases indirect overhead costs, optimizing total project cost.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Project Crashing is a systematic network optimization technique applied when a project must be expedited to meet an aggressive deadline, avoid liquidated contractual delay penalties, or seize commercial market opportunities. Crashing involves injecting extra resources (overtime labor, additional shifts, multi-skilled subcontractors, advanced high-speed machinery) to shorten activity durations at the expense of increased direct expenditures.

1. Cost Concepts in Time-Cost Trade-Off: - Direct Costs: Costs directly assigned to specific work activities (labor wages, overtime pay, equipment leasing, fuel). Direct costs increase as project duration is compressed (crashed). - Indirect Costs: Overhead expenses tied to the total chronological duration of the project (site supervision salaries, trailer rentals, security, insurance, administrative overhead, and penalty liquidated damages). Indirect costs decrease linearly as project duration is shortened: $$\text{Total Indirect Cost} = \text{Indirect Cost Rate per Day } (C_{ind}) \times \text{Total Project Duration } (T)$$ - Total Project Cost: The sum of direct and indirect costs: $$\text{Total Project Cost} = \text{Direct Costs} + \text{Indirect Costs}$$ As project duration is compressed from its normal schedule, total direct costs rise while total indirect costs fall. The optimal project duration is the point where the Total Project Cost reaches its absolute minimum.

2. Normal and Crash Parameters: For every activity $(i \to j)$: - Normal Time ($NT$): Standard activity duration under normal working conditions. - Normal Cost ($NC$): The direct cost associated with completing the task in normal time $NT$. - Crash Time ($CT$): The absolute minimum duration into which the activity can be compressed regardless of additional resources. - Crash Cost ($CC$): The direct cost incurred when completing the task in crash time $CT$.

3. Mathematical Formulation of Cost Slope: Assuming a linear relationship between duration compression and cost increase between normal and crash limits, the Cost Slope represents the additional direct cost incurred per unit time saved (e.g., $\text{INR}$ per day): $$\mathbf{\text{Cost Slope} = \frac{\Delta \text{Cost}}{\Delta \text{Time}} = \frac{\text{Crash Cost } (CC) - \text{Normal Cost } (NC)}{\text{Normal Time } (NT) - \text{Crash Time } (CT)} = \frac{CC - NC}{NT - CT}}$$ The maximum permissible crash duration for any activity is: $$\Delta t_{\max} = NT - CT$$

4. Step-by-Step Algorithmic Procedure for Project Crashing: 1. Construct the project network under Normal Times. Determine earliest and latest event times, identify the Critical Path, and compute total normal project duration $T$ and initial Total Direct Cost ($\sum NC$). 2. Calculate the Cost Slope for all activities in the project. 3. Identify the Critical Activities: Activities lying on the critical path. Non-critical activities have positive float; crashing them increases direct costs without shortening overall project duration. 4. Select the Critical Activity with the Lowest Cost Slope: Expedite this activity first to achieve the cheapest possible duration reduction. 5. Crash the selected activity by the maximum permissible amount until: - Its crash limit is reached ($t = CT$), or - A previously non-critical path becomes parallel critical (creating multiple concurrent critical paths). 6. When Multiple Critical Paths Emerge: To shorten the project further, one must crash: - An activity common to all critical paths, OR - A combination of activities (one from each independent critical path) such that the sum of their individual cost slopes is minimized. 7. Repeat the crashing cycle step-by-step, recalculating Total Cost ($Direct + Indirect$) at each iteration, until no further crashing is technically possible or until the cost slope of the next crash exceeds the daily indirect cost savings. The iteration yielding the absolute minimum Total Project Cost represents the optimal economic schedule.
